<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Tailored for all experience levels

**Speaker Notes:**

 Welcome to Disaster Recovery Step by Step with Templates. In today’s fast-moving and interconnected world, unexpected disruptions—whether from cyberattacks, natural disasters, or technical failures—can halt operations and cause lasting damage. 

This course empowers you to build a practical, structured disaster recovery (DR) plan that your organization can rely on when it matters most. 

You won’t just learn the theory—you’ll actually develop your own plan step by step using downloadable templates and real-world use cases. This course follows a clear, actionable path. 

Each step represents a stage in the disaster recovery planning lifecycle, starting from foundational knowledge and moving all the way to building, testing, and improving your recovery strategy. 

With every step, you’ll complete activities using templates that help solidify the lessons learned. 

To bring these ideas to life, we’ll use CloudCore Technologies Inc., a model SaaS company that depends on the continuous availability of its systems and services. CloudCore will appear throughout the course to show how each task and template looks in practice. You can use this course whether you’re working alone, leading a DR team, or preparing for a compliance requirement. 

You’ll walk away with tools and templates you can immediately apply in your workplace. 

Whether you’re responsible for IT systems, business continuity, security, or operations, this course will give you the clarity, confidence, and materials you need to prepare for disruptions and reduce downtime. Let’s begin the journey toward making your organization more resilient, reliable, and prepared.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="4000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="2853E8"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Course Objectives and Learning Approach

**Speaker Notes:**

This course is designed to help you move from uncertainty to clarity when it comes to disaster recovery. 

The objective is not only to understand the terminology and requirements of a disaster recovery plan but to actually build one using templates and repeatable steps. Our learning approach is built on practical application. You won’t be asked to memorize definitions—you’ll be applying methods and using templates to create something real.

By the end of the course, you will be able to define key concepts such as RTO, RPO, and MTPD; conduct a business impact analysis (BIA); assess and prioritize risks; design tailored recovery strategies; build a detailed recovery plan; and test and update that plan over time. 

You’ll also learn how to communicate with internal and external stakeholders during a crisis and how to write post-incident reports that lead to meaningful improvements.

Our structured, step-by-step approach makes it easier to follow along and apply lessons to your own organization. You’ll be guided by a realistic company scenario—CloudCore Technologies—and supported by templates designed for real use. 

Each step builds upon the previous one so you’ll create your plan progressively as the course unfolds. 

This hands-on, template-driven approach ensures you leave the course with something valuable and applicable, not just theoretical knowledge.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Assignments simulate real DR planning

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Logical flow from theory to practice

**Speaker Notes:**

The course is structured as a sequence of eleven clear and progressive steps, each representing a vital phase in the disaster recovery planning lifecycle. 

It starts by laying the groundwork with foundational knowledge, helping you understand what disaster recovery is, why it matters, and how it fits within the broader context of organizational resilience. 

From there, the course moves into the practical aspects of initiating the DR planning process, including setting the scope, forming a team, and establishing objectives. Once the foundation is laid, you’ll conduct a business impact analysis to identify critical functions and define recovery priorities, followed by a detailed risk assessment to identify and prioritize potential threats. As you move forward, the course guides you through the process of selecting appropriate disaster recovery strategies based on your analysis. 

You’ll then learn how to document these strategies and procedures in a structured disaster recovery plan, making use of provided templates and examples from our model company, CloudCore Technologies. The course continues by helping you design communication protocols, ensuring you know how to keep stakeholders informed and aligned during a disruption. You’ll also learn how to plan, execute, and evaluate various types of DR tests to validate your plan’s effectiveness. Beyond planning and testing, the course addresses how to respond in the aftermath of an actual disaster. You’ll learn how to manage the incident, document what happened, and extract lessons that feed back into the plan. 

The course concludes with a capstone project where you put everything together, applying what you’ve learned to build a complete, professional-grade disaster recovery plan using the templates provided.

Each step includes practical lesson content, downloadable templates, and activities that connect directly to the CloudCore Technologies use case. 

This structured, example-driven approach helps you see exactly how each task is performed and allows you to build your DR plan gradually and with confidence, rather than attempting to create it all at once. 

The format ensures the learning process is manageable, practical, and repeatable for use in your real-world environment.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Uses cloud and third-party services

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Templates pre-filled with CloudCore data

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Ensures DR planning works in practice, not just in theory

**Speaker Notes:**

 To bring the course content to life, we’ll use a fictional but realistic company: CloudCore Technologies Inc. CloudCore is a medium-sized SaaS company based in Austin, Texas. 

It provides cloud-based productivity, accounting, and CRM software to small and mid-sized businesses across North America. 

With over 10,000 customers and a 24/7 availability model, CloudCore depends on uninterrupted access to its applications and customer data. 

Even short periods of downtime can affect revenue, customer trust, and contractual obligations. The company has 80 full-time employees spread across software development, IT support, product management, and customer service. 

CloudCore hosts its platform on a combination of AWS infrastructure and managed third-party services. It also maintains its own internal network and support tools. Given its reliance on cloud systems and continuous access, disaster recovery is not just an IT issue—it’s a business survival priority.

You’ll work with CloudCore throughout the course as a guiding example. All templates are filled in using CloudCore’s structure, data, systems, and risks. 

This ensures you see how DR planning works in practice, not just in theory. Whether or not you’re in a software company, the structure of CloudCore’s challenges and planning process will likely mirror many of your own.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Calibri" panose="020F0502020204030204" pitchFamily="34" charset="0"/></a:rPr><a:t>All downloadable and editable

**Speaker Notes:**

To streamline your disaster recovery planning, we’ve included a full set of professional-grade templates. These tools are designed to help you save time, stay organized, and produce consistent, audit-ready documentation. Each template aligns with a step in the course, and you’ll be guided through how to use them as we go.

You’ll receive a complete Disaster Recovery Plan Template to organize all sections of your plan, from scope and objectives to procedures and responsibilities. 

The Business Impact Analysis Template helps you assess which business functions and systems are most critical, and define recovery time objectives (RTOs) and recovery point objectives (RPOs). 

A Risk Assessment Matrix and Risk Register allow you to score, prioritize, and monitor threats and vulnerabilities across IT and operations. 

To define technical and procedural strategies, the Recovery Strategy Worksheet walks you through available options, backup choices, and system mapping.

For operational planning and validation, the DR Testing Checklist and Communication Plan Template help you manage stakeholder coordination, simulate scenarios, and notify the right people at the right time. 

Finally, the Post-Incident Report Template is used to document any actual recovery event and assess opportunities for improvement.

All templates are fully editable and will be used throughout the course to build your real or example recovery plan.

### Slide 3

**Slide Content:**

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1600" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="0033E4"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Arial"/><a:sym typeface="Arial"/></a:rPr><a:t>Disaster Recovery Step by Step

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="4000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Arial"/><a:sym typeface="Arial"/></a:rPr><a:t>What Is Disaster Recovery?

Aspect

Description

Purpose

Restore systems, data, and services post-disruption

Focus

IT systems, data, applications, infrastructure

Triggered by

Natural, cyber, human, or technical disasters

Common Components

Backups, RTO/RPO, recovery teams, documentation

Output

Documented DR plan with clear procedures

Beneficiaries

All organizations, from startups to enterprises

**Speaker Notes:**

Disaster recovery (DR) is a structured process that enables organizations to recover from disruptive incidents and restore normal operations as quickly as possible. 

These incidents can be caused by natural events, cyberattacks, power failures, hardware breakdowns, or human error. DR encompasses planning, documentation, response, and recovery actions tailored to the organization’s infrastructure and business needs.

Unlike ad hoc responses, DR is intentional and pre-defined. It includes setting recovery priorities, defining objectives such as Recovery Time Objective (RTO) and Recovery Point Objective (RPO), and creating procedures to resume operations. DR goes hand-in-hand with preventive measures like backups and system redundancy. Importantly, DR is not just about technology—it supports business continuity by ensuring critical systems and data are recovered in a timely and organized manner. Without DR, companies risk financial losses, regulatory penalties, and reputational damage. At CloudCore Technologies, for example, a failure to recover their cloud-based services could leave thousands of customers unable to access essential tools, leading to contract breaches and customer attrition.

DR plans must be documented, tested, and updated regularly. 

The scope of each plan depends on the organization’s size, industry, and risk profile. However, all organizations—regardless of size—need a DR strategy, even if it starts with basic steps.

## Lesson 03: Understanding the Big Picture

### Slide 1

**Slide Content:**

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1600" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="0033E4"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Arial"/><a:sym typeface="Arial"/></a:rPr><a:t>Disaster Recovery Step by Step

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="6000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="2853E8"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>DR vs Business Continuity

Feature

Business Continuity

Disaster Recovery

Scope

Whole organization

IT systems and infrastructure

Objective

Maintain operations

Restore lost or damaged systems

Covers

People, processes, facilities

Data, applications, servers

Example Activity

Relocate staff

Recover database from backup

Owned By

Senior Management / Business Units

IT / Security Teams

Timeline

During and after a disruption

Post-disruption

**Speaker Notes:**

Disaster recovery and business continuity are often treated as interchangeable terms, but they serve different purposes. 

Business continuity focuses on keeping the entire business operational during and after a disruption. It addresses broader aspects such as communication with customers, physical workspace relocation, and supply chain management. 

Disaster recovery, on the other hand, is focused specifically on IT systems: restoring applications, data, servers, and infrastructure.

A well-integrated approach involves aligning both. DR is a subset of business continuity. For example, in the case of a ransomware attack, business continuity may involve activating a crisis communication team, while DR focuses on restoring clean data backups and rebuilding compromised servers.

CloudCore Technologies ensures continuity by rerouting customer support operations and providing temporary access to essential functions through alternative platforms. Simultaneously, its DR team works to bring the full application suite back online. This combined approach ensures service availability and trust during recovery. Understanding the distinction helps ensure that both strategic (business) and tactical (technical) elements are covered. It prevents redundancy and clarifies roles during emergencies.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:fld id="{18719C5F-AF85-4ACD-91A6-4198DEE12E57}" type="slidenum"><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1200" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" smtClean="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:pPr marL="0" marR="0" lvl="0" indent="0" algn="r" defTabSz="914400" rtl="0" eaLnBrk="1" fontAlgn="auto" latinLnBrk="0" hangingPunct="1"><a:lnSpc><a:spcPct val="100000"/></a:lnSpc><a:spcBef><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcBef><a:spcAft><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcAft><a:buClrTx/><a:buSzTx/><a:buFontTx/><a:buNone/><a:tabLst/><a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:t>1

### Slide 2

**Slide Content:**

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1600" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="0033E4"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Arial"/><a:sym typeface="Arial"/></a:rPr><a:t>Disaster Recovery Step by Step

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="6000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="2853E8"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Types of Disasters

Category

Examples

DR Considerations

Natural

Floods, earthquakes, hurricanes

Data center redundancy, physical site planning

Technical

Server failure, power outage

Backup power, hardware failover systems

Human

Misconfiguration, accidental deletion

Training, access controls, audit logs

Cyber

Ransomware, DDoS, phishing

Detection tools, encryption, response plans

**Speaker Notes:**

To build an effective disaster recovery plan, it’s essential to understand the different types of disasters your organization might face. These can be broadly grouped into four categories: natural, technical, human, and cyber.

Natural disasters include earthquakes, hurricanes, floods, and wildfires. While they vary by region, their physical damage can disrupt power, damage infrastructure, and cause widespread service outages. 

Technical disasters cover issues like hardware failures, power outages, or system crashes—often overlooked but frequent causes of downtime.

Human errors are one of the most common and underestimated causes of disruption. This includes accidental deletion of critical files, incorrect system configurations, or neglecting routine maintenance. 

Lastly, cyber threats—such as ransomware, data breaches, and denial-of-service attacks—are growing more sophisticated and frequent. These attacks can paralyze systems and compromise sensitive data.

CloudCore, for example, prepares for all of these categories. Their primary application infrastructure is cloud-based and mirrored across regions to mitigate natural and technical risks. They also train staff regularly to avoid human-caused incidents and maintain active monitoring and response systems to detect and react to cyber threats.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:fld id="{18719C5F-AF85-4ACD-91A6-4198DEE12E57}" type="slidenum"><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1200" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" smtClean="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:pPr marL="0" marR="0" lvl="0" indent="0" algn="r" defTabSz="914400" rtl="0" eaLnBrk="1" fontAlgn="auto" latinLnBrk="0" hangingPunct="1"><a:lnSpc><a:spcPct val="100000"/></a:lnSpc><a:spcBef><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcBef><a:spcAft><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcAft><a:buClrTx/><a:buSzTx/><a:buFontTx/><a:buNone/><a:tabLst/><a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:t>2

### Slide 3

**Slide Content:**

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1600" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="0033E4"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Arial"/><a:sym typeface="Arial"/></a:rPr><a:t>Disaster Recovery Step by Step

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="5400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="375FEA"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Why DR Matters to Every Organization

Benefit

Description

Minimizes Downtime

Enables fast recovery of systems and services

Reduces Financial Loss

Prevents revenue loss during outages

Improves Decision-Making

Provides step-by-step response procedures

Builds Trust and Confidence

Reassures clients, partners, and auditors

Supports Compliance

Aligns with legal, regulatory, and contractual obligations

Enhances Preparedness Culture

Makes risk management part of daily operations

**Speaker Notes:**

Disaster recovery is no longer optional—it is an operational requirement in a world where technology underpins nearly every function. 

Even short-term disruptions can lead to lost sales, broken contracts, damaged reputation, and non-compliance with laws or customer agreements. An effective DR plan prepares your organization to respond calmly, restore services quickly, and learn from each incident. Organizations without DR plans often find themselves scrambling during a crisis, relying on informal knowledge or outdated documentation. Recovery becomes slower, more expensive, and riskier. 

A proactive DR approach, supported by tested procedures, helps avoid chaos and empowers teams to make clear decisions when it matters most. At CloudCore Technologies, the risk of downtime is directly tied to customer retention. Their service-level agreements require them to maintain uptime above 99.9%. With a DR plan in place, the company can recover data, notify customers, and switch to backup systems without scrambling.

Moreover, having a DR plan improves trust. Clients, partners, and regulators want assurance that you're ready for the unexpected. For small organizations, even a basic DR checklist and backup protocol can make a substantial difference.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:fld id="{18719C5F-AF85-4ACD-91A6-4198DEE12E57}" type="slidenum"><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1200" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" smtClean="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:pPr marL="0" marR="0" lvl="0" indent="0" algn="r" defTabSz="914400" rtl="0" eaLnBrk="1" fontAlgn="auto" latinLnBrk="0" hangingPunct="1"><a:lnSpc><a:spcPct val="100000"/></a:lnSpc><a:spcBef><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcBef><a:spcAft><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcAft><a:buClrTx/><a:buSzTx/><a:buFontTx/><a:buNone/><a:tabLst/><a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:t>3

## Lesson 04: Laying the Planning Foundation

### Slide 1

**Slide Content:**

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="4000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="2853E8"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Get Executive Buy-In and Define Objectives

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Link DR to business risks and goals

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Include testing and training goals

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>CloudCore example: 4-hour RPO, 12-hour RTO

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Secure commitment from leadership

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Set measurable RTO and RPO targets

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Align DR with compliance and SLA needs

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Maintain DR readiness across key teams

**Speaker Notes:**

The first step in initiating a disaster recovery (DR) planning process is securing active support from executive leadership. Without top-level buy-in, DR projects often stall due to lack of funding, attention, or accountability. Leadership must understand that DR is not just an IT concern—it’s about business resilience, customer trust, and operational continuity.

To earn executive support, demonstrate the risks associated with downtime in terms of lost revenue, regulatory fines, and reputational damage. Use real industry examples or incidents that have affected your organization in the past. 

Also, connect DR outcomes to business goals such as SLA compliance, customer retention, and operational uptime.

Once support is secured, you should define clear and measurable objectives. These might include technical goals like achieving a Recovery Time Objective (RTO) of 12 hours and a Recovery Point Objective (RPO) of four hours. 

Other goals may relate to testing frequency, training coverage, and communication speed during incidents. These objectives ensure the project stays focused and success can be measured.

At CloudCore Technologies, leadership committed to the DR effort after reviewing risks to customer trust and contractual SLAs. Their objectives include maintaining a 4-hour RPO, restoring services within 12 hours, running semi-annual recovery drills, and maintaining DR readiness across key teams.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:fld id="{18719C5F-AF85-4ACD-91A6-4198DEE12E57}" type="slidenum"><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1200" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" smtClean="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:pPr marL="0" marR="0" lvl="0" indent="0" algn="r" defTabSz="914400" rtl="0" eaLnBrk="1" fontAlgn="auto" latinLnBrk="0" hangingPunct="1"><a:lnSpc><a:spcPct val="100000"/></a:lnSpc><a:spcBef><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcBef><a:spcAft><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcAft><a:buClrTx/><a:buSzTx/><a:buFontTx/><a:buNone/><a:tabLst/><a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:t>1

### Slide 2

**Slide Content:**

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1600" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="0033E4"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Arial"/><a:sym typeface="Arial"/></a:rPr><a:t>Disaster Recovery Step by Step

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="6000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="375FEA"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Form the DR Team and Assign Roles

Role

Responsibility

DR Lead

Oversees plan creation and execution

System Recovery Owner

Restores servers, data, and infrastructure

Communications Officer

Manages internal and external communication

HR Liaison

Coordinates with staff and manages logistics

Executive Sponsor

Provides direction and escalates decisions

**Speaker Notes:**

A successful DR plan requires a multidisciplinary team with clearly assigned roles and responsibilities. This team will be responsible for creating, updating, testing, and executing the recovery plan. The team should include members from IT, operations, communications, HR, and executive leadership. Each team member should understand their role and have a designated backup. Having clear accountability helps ensure effective action during emergencies and improves coordination during exercises and testing.

At CloudCore, the DR team includes a DR Lead, System Recovery Owner, Communications Officer, and HR Liaison. 

They meet monthly during the planning phase and conduct quarterly updates post-implementation. Documenting roles and expectations in a team charter helps maintain clarity and engagement.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="4800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="375FEA"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Set Scope and Boundaries of the DR Plan

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Define included systems and locations

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Focus on mission-critical services

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Clarify in-scope vs. out-of-scope items

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Keep initial scope realistic

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Include expected disaster scenarios

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Prioritize customer-impacting functions

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0" err="1"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>CloudCore: product systems in, labs out

**Speaker Notes:**

Defining the scope of your DR plan is critical to keeping the process focused and actionable. 

Scope decisions help clarify which systems, business functions, departments, and locations will be included in the plan—and which will not.

Start by identifying mission-critical systems and operations that support customer service, compliance, or revenue. 

Consider whether your plan should cover just IT systems or include broader functions like facilities and third-party services. 

Be realistic in the first iteration; you can always expand later.

The plan should also specify which disaster scenarios are being addressed. This includes cyberattacks, power outages, data corruption, or natural disasters. 

Not every possible scenario needs a unique plan—grouping them by recovery impact often works better.

CloudCore’s scope includes its main SaaS platform, customer ticketing system, and DevOps infrastructure. Non-production environments, research systems, and internal chat tools are currently out of scope but may be added later.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:fld id="{CBA46BB2-9421-4B71-957B-E87E62F3C4C7}" type="slidenum"><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1200" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" smtClean="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:pPr marL="0" marR="0" lvl="0" indent="0" algn="r" defTabSz="914400" rtl="0" eaLnBrk="1" fontAlgn="auto" latinLnBrk="0" hangingPunct="1"><a:lnSpc><a:spcPct val="100000"/></a:lnSpc><a:spcBef><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcBef><a:spcAft><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcAft><a:buClrTx/><a:buSzTx/><a:buFontTx/><a:buNone/><a:tabLst/><a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:t>3

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Lists roles and responsibilities

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Sets communication and escalation paths

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Improves clarity and accountability

**Speaker Notes:**

The DR Team Charter is a foundational document that outlines the team’s authority, structure, and responsibilities. It formalizes the commitment of members and clarifies expectations across the organization. This is especially helpful in cross-functional teams where daily responsibilities may conflict with DR planning tasks.

The charter should describe the team’s mission, list assigned roles, detail the decision-making hierarchy, and specify how often the team will meet. 

It should also define how communication and escalation will be handled during a disaster.

Approval by leadership ensures alignment with company priorities and provides the team with the authority to act during incidents and testing. 

This is more than a formality—having a signed charter reduces confusion and creates organizational clarity.

At CloudCore, the team charter states that the DR team is empowered to lead testing, manage recovery, and escalate high-impact issues to the CIO. Team members are required to participate in quarterly drills and provide updates to department leads.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Measures disruption impact over time

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Defines RTO and RPO for each process

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Used by CloudCore for SLA-critical systems

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Based on interviews and business data

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Combines business, IT, and finance input

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Supports informed recovery strategy decisions

**Speaker Notes:**

A Business Impact Analysis (BIA) is the process of identifying and evaluating the effects that disruptions to critical business functions would have on an organization. It helps you determine which systems, processes, and resources are essential, how long you can afford to go without them, and what the consequences would be if they were unavailable. 

This step lays the groundwork for recovery priorities and strategy selection. 

The BIA identifies dependencies between business functions and the systems that support them. It calculates both qualitative and quantitative impacts—such as revenue loss, legal penalties, customer dissatisfaction, and safety concerns. 

Once these impacts are understood, the BIA helps define two key recovery metrics: Recovery Time Objective (RTO)—the maximum allowable downtime for a function or system, and Recovery Point Objective (RPO)—the maximum acceptable data loss measured in time.  

CloudCore Technologies uses BIA to identify its highest-priority systems, such as its SaaS platform and customer support ticketing system. Any disruption to these systems results in customer churn, SLA violations, and loss of subscription revenue. 

Through interviews with department heads and data owners, the company establishes realistic RTOs and RPOs and documents which systems must be recovered first. 

The BIA process is structured but collaborative. It’s typically led by the DR team and includes input from business units, IT, and finance. 

The outcome of the BIA informs the scope, sequencing, and design of your recovery strategy.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Look at internal operations such as finance

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Include people, vendors, infrastructure 

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Document in function–dependency matrix 

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1600" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="0033E4"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Arial"/><a:sym typeface="Arial"/></a:rPr><a:t>Disaster Recovery Step by Step

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="4000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="2853E8"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Identify Critical Functions and Dependencies

**Speaker Notes:**

One of the most important aspects of BIA is identifying which business functions are critical to the survival and continued operation of the organization. A critical function is any operation that, if interrupted, would cause significant damage—financially, operationally, legally, or reputationally. 

You must assess each function and its dependencies to understand what needs to be recovered and in what order. 

Begin by listing all major business functions and asking: What would happen if this function were unavailable for one hour? One day? One week? 

Look at internal operations such as finance, customer service, sales, product delivery, and technical support. Evaluate how these functions depend on specific applications, infrastructure, people, and vendors. 

At CloudCore, the customer support team relies heavily on the ticketing platform. If it fails, support cases cannot be processed, SLAs are breached, and customer trust erodes. Similarly, the billing system is crucial for processing renewals—any downtime there leads to lost revenue and errors in subscription management. 

These insights help prioritize system recovery during disaster events. 

Dependencies can be technical (e.g., an app needs a database), procedural (e.g., finance needs access to reports), or human (e.g., remote employees need VPN access). 

All dependencies should be documented in a simple matrix or system map. 

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:fld id="{CBA46BB2-9421-4B71-957B-E87E62F3C4C7}" type="slidenum"><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1200" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" smtClean="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:pPr marL="0" marR="0" lvl="0" indent="0" algn="r" defTabSz="914400" rtl="0" eaLnBrk="1" fontAlgn="auto" latinLnBrk="0" hangingPunct="1"><a:lnSpc><a:spcPct val="100000"/></a:lnSpc><a:spcBef><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcBef><a:spcAft><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcAft><a:buClrTx/><a:buSzTx/><a:buFontTx/><a:buNone/><a:tabLst/><a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:t>3

## Lesson 06: Recovery Requirements and Business Impact

### Slide 1

**Slide Content:**

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1600" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="0033E4"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Arial"/><a:sym typeface="Arial"/></a:rPr><a:t>Disaster Recovery Step by Step

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="6000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="2853E8"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Define RTO and RPO

System

RTO

RPO

SaaS Application

4 hours

15 minutes

Ticketing Platform

6 hours

30 minutes

Billing System

8 hours

1 hour

HR System

48 hours

24 hours

**Speaker Notes:**

RTO and RPO are two critical metrics used in disaster recovery planning. The Recovery Time Objective (RTO) defines how quickly a system or function must be restored after a disruption to avoid unacceptable consequences. The Recovery Point Objective (RPO) defines how much data loss is tolerable, measured in time, from the point of failure to the last good backup.  These metrics are unique for each function or system and should be determined through discussion with process owners and IT teams. 

For example, a financial system might have an RTO of 8 hours and an RPO of 1 hour, meaning the business can tolerate 8 hours of downtime and up to 1 hour of data loss. 

At CloudCore, the RTO for the customer-facing SaaS platform is set at 4 hours due to contractual SLAs, while internal systems such as HR payroll have a more lenient RTO of 48 hours. 

The RPO for customer data is 15 minutes, because even small losses could lead to billing disputes or service errors.  Setting these targets helps you choose the right recovery strategies and technologies. For example, a system with a 5-minute RPO may require continuous replication, while a 12-hour RTO may only need daily backups. Always document and validate these targets with relevant stakeholders.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Estimate revenue loss and compliance fines

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Include downtime effects on staff and customers

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Reputational impacts include customer complaints

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Tie impact to real business metrics

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0" err="1"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>CloudCore: SaaS outage = $5K/hour loss

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Use scoring to rank recovery priority

**Speaker Notes:**

Once functions and recovery objectives are defined, you need to evaluate the actual impact of downtime. 

These impacts typically fall into three categories: financial, operational, and reputational. By understanding the potential consequences, you can justify investments in redundancy, backups, and DR automation.

Financial impacts might include lost sales, delayed invoicing, regulatory fines, or penalties from missed SLAs. 

Operational impacts involve internal delays, interrupted services, staff idle time, and broken workflows. 

Reputational impacts could result from media coverage, customer complaints, or decreased trust among stakeholders.

The BIA should quantify these impacts wherever possible. Even rough estimates—like revenue lost per hour of downtime—can guide cost-benefit decisions. 

For example, if a one-hour outage of the SaaS platform costs $5,000, and an upgraded backup solution costs $2,000 per year, the return on investment is clear.

CloudCore uses a simple scoring matrix to categorize functions by potential impact. Those with “High” financial and operational impact receive top priority during recovery planning.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Document RTO, RPO, and dependencies

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1600" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="0033E4"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Arial"/><a:sym typeface="Arial"/></a:rPr><a:t>Disaster Recovery Step by Step

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="4800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="2853E8"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Use the BIA Template (Walkthrough)

**Speaker Notes:**

To guide your analysis, this course provides a downloadable Business Impact Analysis Template. The template includes fields for documenting business functions, their owners, dependencies, RTO and RPO values, and estimated impact levels. Completing this template ensures consistency across departments and provides a consolidated view of your recovery priorities.

Start by gathering input through interviews, workshops, or questionnaires. Ask department heads to describe key activities and what happens when those activities are disrupted. 

Fill in the function name, owner, description, criticality rating, and required recovery times. Identify dependencies and note whether each function can be performed manually during downtime.

CloudCore’s BIA template includes completed examples for functions like customer support, billing, and DevOps pipelines. 

These examples show how to define impact levels and recovery metrics based on real-world business data.

Once completed, the BIA document becomes a central planning tool. It supports risk assessment, strategy selection, and DR plan structure. 

It should be reviewed at least annually or whenever systems or processes change significantly.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Document system and process vulnerabilities

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Focus on risks relevant to your operations

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0" err="1"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>CloudCore risks: code loss, cloud outages

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Vulnerabilities include lack of offline backups

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Use workshops or checklists to gather input

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Include cyber, physical, technical, and human risks

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Record findings in a risk register

**Speaker Notes:**

After identifying critical functions and dependencies in the BIA, the next step is to understand what could disrupt those functions. 

Risk assessment begins by identifying threats—external or internal events that could cause harm—and vulnerabilities—weaknesses in your systems or processes that make those threats more likely to succeed.

Common threats include natural disasters, cyberattacks, equipment failure, and insider misuse. Vulnerabilities might involve outdated software, untested backups, poor access controls, or a lack of employee awareness. Together, these elements define your risk landscape. The goal is not to document every conceivable risk but to focus on realistic, high-impact scenarios. 

These scenarios should be relevant to your business model, infrastructure, location, and industry. 

At CloudCore, threats include cyberattacks on their SaaS platform, accidental code deletion, and cloud provider outages. 

Vulnerabilities include lack of offline backups, limited geographic redundancy, and over-reliance on a single service provider.

Engage stakeholders across departments to brainstorm risks. Use structured workshops, checklists, and interviews. 

Categorizing threats as physical, technical, or human can help keep the discussion organized. 

Once identified, threats and vulnerabilities are logged in a risk register for evaluation.

### Slide 2

**Slide Content:**

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1600" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="0033E4"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Arial"/><a:sym typeface="Arial"/></a:rPr><a:t>Disaster Recovery Step by Step

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="4800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="2853E8"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Assess Risk Likelihood and Impact

Threat

**Speaker Notes:**

Once risks have been identified, the next step is to assess the likelihood of each threat occurring and the impact it would have on your operations. 

Likelihood is typically rated as low, medium, or high based on past incidents, industry trends, and system exposure. 

Impact is also ranked based on the potential damage to operations, finances, customers, and compliance.

Use a risk matrix to visualize and prioritize these risks. Risks that are both high in likelihood and high in impact should be addressed first. Those with low impact and low likelihood may be noted but do not require immediate attention.

At CloudCore, a DDoS attack on their platform is considered high impact and medium likelihood, making it a top-priority risk. 

In contrast, a fire in their office would be high impact but low likelihood due to fire prevention measures, so it receives lower priority.

Quantifying risk in this structured way helps guide decision-making and ensures resources are allocated efficiently. It also supports communication with executives, who may not be familiar with technical details but can understand business-level consequences.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Track implementation of controls over time

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Update register as systems or risks change

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Supports audits and compliance reporting

**Speaker Notes:**

After evaluating threats, the next step is to prioritize and document them in a risk register. The risk register serves as a central repository for all identified threats, their risk scores, and mitigation plans. 

It allows DR planners to stay organized and supports tracking of mitigation progress over time.

Each entry in the risk register should include the name of the threat, affected systems or processes, likelihood, impact, priority, existing controls, and recommended actions. This structured documentation ensures transparency and provides a clear action plan for reducing exposure to the most significant risks. 

CloudCore’s risk register lists all risks affecting the availability of their SaaS application, including external attacks, code failures, and vendor disruptions. 

Each is assigned a priority, and mitigation steps are tracked—such as implementing Web Application Firewalls or enabling

multi-region failover.

This document should be reviewed regularly—at least annually—or when major systems, vendors, or threats change. 

It also serves as valuable input for audit or compliance activities.

## Lesson 08: Advanced Risk Modeling and Planning

### Slide 1

**Slide Content:**

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1800" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Calibri" panose="020F0502020204030204" pitchFamily="34" charset="0"/></a:rPr><a:t>Supports secure recovery planning

**Speaker Notes:**

For organizations with complex systems—particularly those delivering digital products—threat modeling can provide deeper insight into how specific systems may be exploited. 

Threat modeling focuses on identifying potential attacker goals, system weaknesses, and how those weaknesses could be used to compromise confidentiality, integrity, or availability.

One popular method is the STRIDE model, which categorizes threats as Spoofing, Tampering, Repudiation, Information disclosure, Denial of service, and Elevation of privilege. It helps teams analyze how users, systems, and data flow through an application—and where threats might emerge.

At CloudCore, the DR team applied STRIDE to their API authentication module. 

They identified potential spoofing and tampering risks that could disable login services. This analysis informed the need for multi-factor authentication and audit logging.

While threat modeling is optional for basic DR planning, it’s highly recommended for cloud services, SaaS platforms, and regulated environments. 

It allows for precise mapping of technical threats to recovery strategies.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Makes risk data easy to visualize

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Supports planning, audits, and reporting

**Speaker Notes:**

To help organize your findings, use the Risk Assessment Template provided in this course. 

This downloadable document allows you to systematically list threats, rate their risk, and track responses. 

It includes columns for threat description, affected systems, likelihood, impact, current controls, proposed actions, and owner. Begin by transferring the risks identified during your workshops or interviews. Then assign values for likelihood and impact using your predefined rating system. 

Assign a risk level (e.g., High, Medium, Low), and describe any mitigation already in place. Add action items for unresolved risks and assign them to team members with due dates.

The CloudCore example in the template shows risks like “Cloud provider failure” and “Malicious employee action,” each rated and assigned to owners for action. By walking through this document, you'll get a clear view of which risks require immediate attention and what can be managed over time.

This template helps you move from abstract analysis to concrete planning and allows for easy sharing with leadership, auditors, or external consultants.

### Slide 3

**Slide Content:**

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1600" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="0" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="0033E4"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="Arial"/><a:sym typeface="Arial"/></a:rPr><a:t>Disaster Recovery Step by Step

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="5400" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:srgbClr val="375FEA"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Map Systems to Recovery Priorities

System

Tier

RTO

Strategy Type

SaaS Platform

1

4 hours

Hot standby + failover

Customer Support Ticketing

1

6 hours

Cloud replication

CI/CD Pipeline (DevOps Tools)

2

24 hrs

Snapshot backup

HR Portal

3

48 hrs

Cold site recovery

**Speaker Notes:**

After completing your BIA and risk assessment, you should have a clear understanding of your most critical systems, their recovery requirements, and the risks they face. Now it’s time to map each system to a suitable recovery priority. This step ensures that when a disruption occurs, your team knows what needs to be recovered first, how quickly, and in what order.

Start by listing systems in descending order of criticality—based on their RTOs, RPOs, and impact levels identified earlier. Then, match each system to the appropriate recovery strategy. For instance, high-priority systems that support customer transactions might need high-availability architecture, while lower-priority systems like internal knowledge bases could rely on slower, manual restoration.

At CloudCore, the SaaS application, customer support platform, and payment system are classified as Tier 1 (highest priority). 

DevOps tools and employee timesheets are Tier 2, while training portals are Tier 3. This categorization guides how resources—like backup servers, bandwidth, and staff—are allocated during recovery. Grouping systems by recovery tiers simplifies planning and allows you to apply the right level of investment where it matters most.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Warm/cold sites fit lower-tier systems

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Cloud replication ensures data availability

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Snapshots ideal for longer RTO systems

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0" err="1"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>CloudCore uses a hybrid approach

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Align tech strategy with business priority

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:r><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="2000" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" dirty="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="white"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:t>Choose based on risk, cost, and need

**Speaker Notes:**

Once systems are prioritized, you can select the most suitable recovery methods. These approaches depend on system criticality, recovery time needs, budget, and the organization's technical capacity. 

Some systems will require fast failover mechanisms, while others may use basic backups and manual procedures. Here are some common recovery options:

Hot Site: A fully operational environment running in parallel with the primary system. Enables instant failover but is costly.Warm Site: A partially prepared environment that requires some setup post-disruption. Cold Site: A backup location or infrastructure that needs to be configured from scratch. Cheapest, but slowest.Cloud Replication: Continuously mirrors your data and configurations to another cloud region or service.Snapshots & Backups: Suitable for systems with longer RTOs. Simple, low-cost, and widely used.CloudCore uses a mix of strategies. 

Its production SaaS platform replicates data across regions with hot standby enabled, while internal systems rely on weekly backups and slower restoration timelines.

The goal is to match strategy to system need—don’t overspend protecting systems that aren’t mission-critical, and don’t cut corners on customer-facing services.

<a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:fld id="{95DF0372-9F23-4F11-8187-0DE2449A2192}" type="slidenum"><a:rPr kumimoji="0" lang="en-US" sz="1200" b="0" i="0" u="none" strike="noStrike" kern="1200" cap="none" spc="0" normalizeH="0" baseline="0" noProof="0" smtClean="0"><a:ln><a:noFill/></a:ln><a:solidFill><a:prstClr val="black"/></a:solidFill><a:effectLst/><a:uLnTx/><a:uFillTx/><a:latin typeface="Aptos" panose="02110004020202020204"/><a:ea typeface="+mn-ea"/><a:cs typeface="+mn-cs"/></a:rPr><a:pPr marL="0" marR="0" lvl="0" indent="0" algn="r" defTabSz="914400" rtl="0" eaLnBrk="1" fontAlgn="auto" latinLnBrk="0" hangingPunct="1"><a:lnSpc><a:spcPct val="100000"/></a:lnSpc><a:spcBef><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcBef><a:spcAft><a:spcPts val="0"/></a:spcAft><a:buClrTx/><a:buSzTx/><a:buFontTx/><a:buNone/><a:tabLst/><a:defRPr/></a:pPr><a:t>1
